### 2026-10-11: Where the real account's error comes from: method, written before the script exists
- **Why.** Proposals for lump income target the synthetic student personas, not my account, so I redo the synthetic diagnosis (2026-10-10) for my account, without an answer key. My real account is development data, as recorded; I design this while looking at it, so nothing here is an evaluation.
- **What it is.** `python -m eval.diagnose_real`, run by me on `main`, on the current forecaster. It opens the database read-only, runs the same origins as the real backtest (weekly Mondays, 8 weeks of history behind, 30 observed days after), and prints summary numbers only: no row, date, text or name, and it writes no file.
- **The forecast it explains** is the app's own at each origin (weekday method, 500 futures, seed 0, series and decisions as of the origin, rule zero). It explains the **mean** of the futures (a mean splits into parts, a percentile does not), and prints the middle future's error beside it.
- **What happened, by part.** Every row posted in the 30 days after the origin goes in exactly one part, by the first rule that fits: (1) netted holds (a hold and its reversal); (2) bank fees and their reversals; (3) rows of a recurring series, taken from the series as they stand 30 days after the origin (a label for what happened; the forecast never sees it); (4) money in of a kind the forecaster counts (income or transfer type), split by the big-deposit cutoff the forecast had at the origin: under it, "irregular deposits" (the weekly resampling stands for them); at or above it, "big deposits" (set aside, never forecast); (5) other money in: card returns and corrections count as negative everyday spending, any other credit is "other credits"; (6) money out, split by the big-payment cutoff at the origin: under it, everyday spending (card, cash, transfers and bill payments in no series); at or above it, "big payments" (set aside); any other debit is "other debits". Whatever the balance changed that the rows do not explain is "unexplained" (it should be zero, since the balance chain reconciles).
- **What the forecast expected, by part.** Recurring: the payments placed on their nominal days. Everyday spending and irregular deposits: the expected value of the weekly resampling (day k of the horizon takes the average of that weekday over the last 13 weeks' weeks, as in the synthetic diagnosis). Fees: the daily rate. Big deposits, big payments, netted holds, other credits and debits: zero, because the forecast leaves them out. "Leftover" is the mean of the futures minus the sum of these parts: it is the date jitter inside the futures and rounding, and it is shown, not hidden.
- **The two errors, both expected minus happened (negative = the forecast was too pessimistic).** (a) The 30-day change of the balance: the parts add up exactly to the mean future's error. (b) The lowest balance: the median over futures of each future's lowest balance, minus the realized lowest. To split it, take *k*, the day of the realized lowest; the parts are each part's error in the cumulated change up to day *k*, plus the leftover at *k*, plus "the low point": the median of the futures' lowest minus the mean future on day *k* (the lowest of random paths sits below their mean, and the futures' low days differ from the realized one). The pieces add up to the lowest-balance error exactly.
- **Recurring items, split further.** Placed payments and the series rows that happened are matched one to one within a series, nearest day first, within 10 days. Unmatched placed payments are **missed** (placed, nothing came); unmatched real rows are **extra** (including payments of a series first detected after the origin); matched pairs give **wrong amount** (placed minus real) and **wrong date** (counted, with the average days off; its effect on the 30-day change is zero by construction, and at the realized low day it is the part of the error caused by the date alone). The four add up to the recurring part at day 30 and at day *k*.
- **Reported.** For all origins and for the ordinary ones (balance at the origin not below my buffer): n origins; for each part the mean and the median error, the mean absolute error, and the share of origins where it is the biggest absolute part, for the 30-day change and for the lowest balance. Then the irregular deposits (the stream the weekly resampling draws from): counts by size band (under $200, $200 to $500, $500 up to the big cutoff, at or above it) for the last 365 days and for the whole history, by type, how regularly they arrive (the share of weeks and of 30-day windows with at least one deposit in each band, the median gap in days, the share of the money in) and what the forecaster expects from the weekly draw per 30 days against what arrived.
- **Checks, fixed now.** On synthetic personas, with tests: the happened parts plus unexplained equal the realized change on every day; the expected parts plus the leftover equal the mean future on every day; the lowest-balance pieces add up exactly; the recurring split adds up at day 30 and at day *k*; rewriting every row after the origin changes none of the expected numbers (rule zero); no output line contains a date, amount from a row, or text. The script prints no rows: only the first and last origin count and these summaries.
- **Not claimed.** Part labels for what happened use rows up to 30 days after the origin and flows that depend on later rows only through netting (a hold whose reversal posts after the window is not netted). A bill payment that was set aside as a pass-through at the origin cannot be told apart from a big payment by size alone (rows after the origin cannot be paired with earlier deposits, so this only affects rows under the cutoff that an earlier deposit funded: they count as everyday spending). Date jitter is only in the leftover. One account, 92 origins that overlap: the counts of origins are not independent periods.

### 2026-10-11: Real-account error breakdown: result (qualitative) and open items for later
- Result, my account, 92 weekly origins, summary numbers run by me, none written here: the breakdown says which parts of the error to work on next (below).
- This is development data. It says where to look next, not that a fix works.
- **For later, not now (1): own-account transfers in are not income.** Transfers between my own accounts are my own decisions (I move money when I choose to), not income that arrives by itself, so they should not be drawn as if they were. Needs its own treatment (an own-account stream, or left out and shown as an assumption).
- **For later, not now (2): recurring placement misses many payments.** Some placed payments did not happen, and some real payments of known series were not placed (missed and extra). Needs a look at how series are detected and placed (rent windows, series first seen late, payments that move).

### 2026-10-11: Big money that keeps coming: what the cutoffs are, a diagnostic, and a proposal (branch `income-stream`; proposal not approved yet, nothing built)
**Which cutoff each rule uses (from the code, checked line by line).** The forecaster (`_split_large` in `lookahead/forecast/spending.py`) and the lump detection (`lump_stages`, on the `proposals` branch) use the **same rule**: a row is big if it is at least $500 and at least 5 times the typical (median) row of **its own direction** over the last 13 weeks, where the typical row is that of the deposits (or payments) themselves when there are at least 5 in the window, and only otherwise that of everything. Neither uses "a typical row of any kind" when there are enough deposits. They are the same function written twice (three times, with the real-account breakdown script); they do not differ in rule. What differs is **the date they are evaluated at**: the forecaster at every origin on its own 13 weeks, the lump diagnostic once, at the last posting date, applied to a whole year of rows. The cutoff moves with the typical deposit: when most recent deposits are small it sits at the $500 floor and a deposit of a few hundred dollars above it is "big"; when deposits are larger it rises. So a deposit that is under the cutoff today can have been set aside when it arrived, and the diagnostic and the breakdown can both be right and disagree.

**Diagnostic (counts and summary numbers only; `python -m eval.big_money`, run by me).** Per origin of the real backtest, from rows posted by then: the big-deposit and big-payment cutoffs the forecaster used (median, smallest, largest over origins; the share of origins at the $500 floor; the share where the deposits' own typical row was used) and the cutoff at the last posting date; how many deposits and payments the forecaster set aside in the 13 weeks before each origin (as a large deposit or payment, or as a pass-through), per origin: median, largest, share of origins with at least one. Then, over the whole history, every deposit and payment of at least $500 in no series, counted by whether it was **big on the day it arrived** (cutoff computed from rows posted by that day), by size band ($500 to $1,000, $1,000 to $2,500, $2,500 to $5,000, $5,000 and up), with the share of the money; and, for the ones that were big on arrival, how many had 0, 1, or 2 or more **earlier rows of similar size** (same direction, in no series, within half to double the size, in the 365 days before it), by count and share of the money. That last table is the size of what the proposal below would change. No row, date, amount of a row or text is printed.

**Proposal (not approved).** Today the size rule is the only test: any big row is set aside and never forecast. That keeps a one-off from repeating every week (the $16,000 tuition case, which must keep working) but also removes money that keeps arriving at a similar size.
1. **One cutoff function.** `big_cutoffs()` in `spending.py`, used by the forecaster, the lump detection and the scripts. A pure refactor: golden tests on synthetic personas must give identical streams before and after. Whether the 5x-the-typical-row yardstick should be a different yardstick is a separate question (it drifts with recent small rows); it is not changed here.
2. **A second test, "keeps coming".** A big row is **recurring-size** if, among the rows of its direction posted by the forecast date, in no series, within the 365 days before it, there are at least 2 others of similar size (half to double). Two others, so three in all: below that a pattern is not shown. Everything else big stays set aside as today (genuinely rare: one-offs, pass-throughs).
3. **Treatment of recurring-size big deposits.** Not in the weekly block draw (so nothing repeats weekly) but drawn as **random arrivals at their own observed rate and sizes**, as in the income prototype: rate = such rows / days observed in the last 365, one arrival at most a day, size drawn from the observed sizes, own random stream, counted as irregular deposits (so "no other money expected" turns them off). Rule zero: only rows posted by the forecast date. Pass-throughs stay set aside (their outflow is set aside too).
4. **Treatment of recurring-size big payments.** Same test. The same arrivals, **only when there are at least 6 a year**; fewer (tuition: 2 or 3 a year) stay set aside until the scheduled placement for repeated large outflows (step b of the proposals plan) exists, because a random $19,000 payment in a quarter of the futures would give a standing chance of a shortfall that a known semester date does not. Open for my decision: use the same floor of 3 for payments and accept that.
5. **Unchanged:** the $500 floor and the 5x multiple, bills and series, pass-through pairing, the weekly draw for everything under the cutoff, fees.

**Evaluation.**
- *Synthetic, development personas (20, 40 origins, 500 futures, seed 42, raw chances, the original episode definition, as the earlier results).* Same table as the income prototype: error at 7, 14, 30 days and skill, lowest-balance bias and typical miss (all windows, and windows with and without a tuition payment), 80 percent coverage and width, whole-path coverage, Brier overall and per archetype, alerts at the provisional rule; the current forecaster beside the new one, n (personas, origins, shortfall episodes) with every number. **No regression:** personas with no recurring-size big row (checked by count) must give identical results to the digit; a made-up single $16,000 payment or deposit must leave the futures exactly as today (test); no archetype may get worse in 30-day error by more than noise I state before running (the mean absolute error of the current forecaster is the yardstick).
- *Prediction, written first:* the windows with irregular family money and no tuition become less pessimistic (as the prototype did); tuition windows do not change; the band at 30 days gets wider where arrivals are added; SAL, SEM, TIGHT and DHOME are unchanged. If the 30-day error or the lowest-balance bias gets worse for most archetypes, the proposal is dropped, not tuned.
- *My real account (I run it; counts and summaries only; development, not evaluation):* the diagnostic above first, and its table decides whether this is worth building (if the recurring-size share of the set-aside money is small, stop). Then the breakdown script (`eval.diagnose_real`) on the new forecaster: the big-deposit part of the 30-day error and the lowest-balance bias, before and after; the chance of going below $0 with a $0 buffer against how often it happened; the number of recurring-size rows and of arrivals per origin.
- **Order:** (1) I run the diagnostic; (2) the single cutoff function with golden tests; (3) the rule, with tests; (4) the synthetic comparison; (5) the real-account run. Each step conditional on the one before.
- The `proposals` branch is not touched; the overlap with its lump proposals (by sender, with Looks right / Not this month) is deliberate: this step is the unconfirmed fallback, applied to money that keeps coming whether or not it has a sender name.

### 2026-10-11: Big money that keeps coming: two decisions before the diagnostic is run
- **Payment floor: 6 a year.** Recurring-size big payments come back as random arrivals only when there are at least 6 a year; tuition-like payments (2 or 3 a year) stay set aside until the scheduled placement for repeated large outflows exists. This settles the open point in the proposal above.
- **The diagnostic also splits deposits by the bank's transaction type** (incoming e-Transfer, own-account transfer, credit memo, deposit): of the big deposits that have 2 or more earlier rows of similar size, how much of the money each type makes up. Why: a transfer in from one's own other account often happens because the balance got low, so drawing it at random as if it were income would partly assume savings come to the rescue. If own-account transfers are a large share of the recurring-size money, the arrivals rule must leave them out (they are the "own-account transfers are not income" item above), and the proposal changes before anything is built. Counts and shares only.

### 2026-10-11: Big money that keeps coming: go, and how it is built and evaluated (decided after the real-account diagnostic)
- **Decision: build the proposal as recorded.** The rule stays exactly as written: "keeps coming" means 2 or more earlier rows of similar size (half to double, same direction, in no series, in the 365 days before); deposits come back as random arrivals; payments only when there are 6 or more such payments a year. **The "2 or more" is not changed after seeing the diagnostic.**
- **Own-account transfers in stay in the arrivals pool**, and every real-account result is reported twice: *with* them counted and *without*. "Without" means a transfer in from my own other account is neither a candidate nor an earlier similar row for anything else, and stays set aside as today. The difference between the two columns shows how much of any improvement comes from own-account transfers. (Own-account transfers are not income; the open item above stands. The synthetic personas are reported both ways too.)
- **Built as options, off by default.** `KeepsComing(own_account=True|False)` is passed to `build_forecast`; `None` (the default) leaves every number exactly as it is now, which a test checks. The arrivals are a new input to the simulation with their own random streams, so no other draw moves. Payments' arrivals lower the balance in the futures with and without deposits (they are spending, not deposits); deposits' arrivals count as irregular deposits and "no other money expected" turns them off. A spending cut does not touch them.
- **Details fixed now.** Rate = such rows in the last 365 days divided by the days observed in that window (history shorter than a year: the days it has); one arrival at most a day; size drawn from the observed sizes. A row is a candidate if it is at least the big cutoff of the forecast date, in no series, not netted, and not part of a pass-through (a pass-through is judged first, over the same 365 days, and stays set aside). Rows set aside as before are listed as before; rows that become arrivals are listed separately and are not called "not forecast". Rule zero: only rows posted on or before the forecast date; tested by rewriting every later row.
- **One cutoff function** (`big_cutoffs`), used by the forecaster and by the scripts; a pure refactor, checked against a golden snapshot taken before the change (streams and set-aside events of synthetic personas at many origins) and by the whole existing suite.
- **Synthetic evaluation, as recorded:** the 20 development personas, 40 origins, 500 futures, seed 42, raw chances, the original episode definition. Three runs: off, on with own-account transfers, on without. Table: error at 7/14/30 days and skill, lowest-balance bias and typical miss (all windows, and windows with and without a tuition payment from the generator's answer key), 80 percent coverage and width, whole-path coverage, Brier, provisional-rule alerts, per archetype, n (personas, origins, shortfall episodes) beside every number. Personas that never have a recurring-size big row must be identical to the digit; this is checked from the counts the run saves. **Prediction (unchanged):** windows with irregular family money and no tuition get less pessimistic; tuition windows do not change; the 30-day band gets wider where arrivals are added; SAL, SEM, TIGHT, DHOME unchanged. If the 30-day error or the lowest-balance bias gets worse for most archetypes, the proposal is dropped, not tuned.
- **Real account (I run it; development only):** the backtest on the new forecaster in its own folders (with and without own-account transfers; own buffer and $0), then `eval.diagnose_real` on it, with the new parts "keeps-coming deposits" and "keeps-coming payments", next to the current forecaster's numbers for the big-deposit part.
- **For later: the cutoff drifts.** The big-deposit cutoff is 5 times the typical deposit of the last 13 weeks, so it can swing a long way between origins as the mix of small and large deposits changes. A deposit can be set aside one month and left in the pool the next. The "keeps coming" test makes the outcome less dependent on it, but the yardstick itself (a longer window, a median that ignores small e-Transfers, a size relative to the person's money in per month) is a separate question to settle with data.

### 2026-10-11: Keeps-coming synthetic evaluation: the drop rule made exact, before it is run
- **"Worse" is defined now, before any result.** For an archetype of the development set (ordinary origins), the new forecaster is *worse* on the 30-day error if its mean absolute error of the middle future is more than 1 percent above the current forecaster's, and *worse* on the lowest balance if the absolute value of its mean signed miss is more than 1 percent above the current one's. Any archetype whose records are identical counts as not worse. **The proposal is dropped if, for either measure, more than half of the archetypes (5 or more of the 8) are worse**, for the arrivals with own-account transfers or without them (each is judged on its own). It is not tuned afterwards.
- Tuition windows are the 30 days after an origin that contain a tuition payment in the generator's answer key; "family money, no tuition" windows contain a family deposit or top-up and no tuition payment. The lowest-balance bias is shown for the three kinds of window (tuition, family money without tuition, neither) with n.
- Personas with no arrival at any origin must have records identical to the current forecaster's; the report lists them and checks every forecast field.
- Command: `python -m eval.keeps_coming_eval` (three runs into `data/backtest/keeps-coming-{off,with-own,without-own}/full`, each resumable; a table is written to `docs/results/keeps_coming_2026-10-11.md`).

### 2026-10-11: Keeps coming: synthetic result (development personas; not merged, off by default)
20 development personas, 726 ordinary origins (800 in all), 500 futures, seed 42, raw chances, the original episode definition. Table in `docs/results/keeps_coming_2026-10-11.md`. The drop rule fixed before the run (more than half of the 8 archetypes worse by more than 1 percent) **is not triggered**, with or without own-account transfers.
- **It acts rarely:** at least one arrival stream at 62 of 800 origins (ITIGHT, FREE and a few others). 14 of the 20 personas never get one and are **identical to the digit** to the current forecaster in every forecast field (DHOME-01 and -03, DRENT-01 to -03, IFAM-01 and -03, SAL-01 to -03, SEM-01, TIGHT-01 to -03); the archetype rows of DHOME, DRENT, SAL, SEM and TIGHT show no change to the dollar.
- **Against the written prediction:** windows with family money and no tuition are less pessimistic (ITIGHT and IFAM, n=77: lowest-balance bias -$347 to -$298); tuition windows do not change (+$10); SAL, SEM, TIGHT and DHOME unchanged; the 30-day band is wider (width $849 to $903, coverage 47 to 48 percent). All as predicted, all small.
- **Overall (ordinary origins):** 30-day error $1,737 to $1,727 (skill 0.10 both); lowest-balance bias +$710 to +$716 (typical miss $999 to $994); Brier 0.057 to 0.058. No archetype is worse on the 30-day error. Two are worse on the lowest-balance bias, by the 1 percent rule: ITIGHT ($4,538 to $4,588, in the optimistic direction; I did not look for the cause) and FREE ($73 to $77). Alerts at the provisional rule: recall 26 of 52 to 25 of 52, precision 22 of 31 to 21 of 30, false alerts per 30 days unchanged.
- **With and without own-account transfers give the same synthetic numbers:** the generator's big deposits are credit memos, never own-account transfers, so the synthetic personas say nothing about that switch. Only my account can.
- **A finding that changes how earlier numbers are read: the holiday double-paycheque bug reached 16 of the 20 development personas, not 2.** The "current forecaster" column here ($594 / $1,058 / $1,737 at 7 / 14 / 30 days) is lower than the first development backtest and the overnight tables ($667 / $1,204 / $1,901). Checked: with the fix switched off, IFAM-01 and SEM-01 reproduce the October 9 records at all 40 origins exactly; with it on, 16 personas change at 4, 8 or 12 origins (one per affected series, around the New Year and Easter holidays; IFAM and SEM have three affected series each). The overnight diagnosis counted only the pay series of two personas. **So the first development backtest, the diagnosis, the calibration, the threshold curve, the income prototype comparison and the Model report's synthetic column were all computed with the bug and are not comparable with anything computed after the fix.** Nothing was recomputed; the Model report and the README placeholders must be refreshed from a new run before they are quoted. This run, the three arms, is consistent within itself (all three have the fix).
- **How I read it.** The rule does what it was written to do and costs little, but on synthetic data it has almost nothing to act on, so this is no evidence that it helps. Whether it matters is a question for my account. Not merged; the app is unchanged (the switch is off).

### 2026-10-11: Keeps coming: real-account result and the decision to adopt it as the default on `income-stream`
- **What I ran (my account, development data; summary numbers only, none written here).** A refreshed baseline of the current forecaster, then the keeps-coming forecaster with own-account transfers counted and without them, each with my buffer and at $0, and the error breakdown for each.
- **The refreshed baseline is identical to the old one:** the real comparisons never had the problem the synthetic ones did.
- **With own-account transfers counted, on my own account:** run by me; the results are not written here.
- **Decision: adopt keeps coming, with own-account transfers counted, as the default of the forecaster on the branch `income-stream`.** `build_forecast` and the backtest and breakdown commands use it unless told otherwise (`keeps=None`, or `--keeps-coming off`, gives the old behaviour exactly). Why: on the synthetic personas nothing is harmed (14 of 20 personas are identical to the digit and the drop rule I fixed beforehand did not trigger). **This decision used my real account as development data**: I chose it after seeing the real result, so that result is not an evaluation and the held-out personas are the only test left. It stays unmerged into `main` for now.
- **Why not merge now:** the `proposals` branch touches the same forecaster files. When that run is finished I will merge one branch at a time, with the full suite between them.
- **Known costs, stated plainly.** The 30-day band is now wide.
- **Candidate experiments for later, to be pre-registered before any result is looked at (not started):**
  1. **Repeated large payments by calendar position:** a payment about a year after the same payment last year (same payee, same time of year), instead of "last payment plus the median gap". The proposals run found that last plus median gap fails for tuition paid in two terms.
  2. **Blending the forecast with "the balance stays the same"** for accounts where that is a strong baseline (on my account the forecast doesn't beat it yet). The weight would have to be chosen on development personas only and decided before the held-out run.
  3. **Lumpy arrivals make the middle future pessimistic while the mean is close.** Decide, before looking at new results, what the app should show (the middle future, the mean, or the range with the likelihood of dipping) and what the metrics should score (the middle future's error, the mean's, or the chance of dipping), because choosing after the fact would pick whichever looks best.
### 2026-10-10: Next forecaster step agreed: proposals for lump income and repeated large payments, and a stricter "caught"
- Branch `proposals`; the plan for the first overnight run is `docs/plans/overnight_2026-10-11.md`. Nothing below is built yet; each method is written here, in full, before the code that computes it runs.
- **a) Lump income as proposals** (by sender: typical size, typical gap; "Looks right" / "Not this month"; unconfirmed lumps fall back to random arrivals from their own rate and sizes). **b) Repeated large outflows** (tuition and the like) are placed by default on their expected date, labelled "detected, not confirmed", with "Looks right" / "Not this time"; with one payment seen, a planned item prefilled from it is offered instead. b) reverses the 2026-10-02 choice "no semester cadence".
- **c) Caught, redefined** (approved with the recording protocol written in the plan): a shortfall episode is caught if an alert was on at an ordinary origin before its first day and within 30 days, whether or not it opened in that window. Both definitions are printed side by side, the threshold is chosen again on raw chances (calibration is shelved), and the definition and threshold are fixed before the held-out run. Seen before the change: the provisional-rule results, the threshold curve on calibrated chances, and the cause of the 15 dips at three personas (one alert open across several dips). The real account has also been seen, so its numbers are development.
- **A separate 24-month persona set, as a new set of results.** Same ids with the prefix `v2L-`, same archetypes, ranges, assignments, buffers and held-out list, history from 2024-10-01 to 2026-09-30, the same 40 weekly origins. It is **not a change to the frozen 12-month mix**, whose personas and results stay as they are, and the two sets are never pooled. Why: with 12 months a tuition payee has been paid once (twice for six personas), so b) cannot be tested. The set's method and acceptance checks are written when it is built, before anything is generated; nothing is adjusted to the checks.
- Held-out personas stay untouched in both sets. Calibration stays shelved. The random lump arrivals of the income prototype are rebuilt on `proposals` from their recorded design, as an option that is off by default; the prototype branch is deleted, and no commit that quoted figures from my own account is carried over.
- Done today on `main`: the holiday double-paycheque fix, the Time Machine what-if sign (positive is money in), and the Model report labels the calibration results "shelved: not used by the app".

### LightGBM spending model (branch `lightgbm-prototype`, experiment only): design, written before training
- **What it predicts:** cumulative everyday spending D(t, h) from the day after the origin t through h days later, the same stream the weekday baseline forecasts (card purchases, cash, transfers and bill payments in no series, less returns; big one-time rows and pass-throughs left out). A direct model per horizon: one row per origin and horizon, h in {7, 14, 21, 30}, the horizon as a feature, and D for the days in between interpolated linearly from D(t, 0) = 0.
- **What happened** (the target): the same stream classified as of t + 30 (recurring series and the big-row rule as of the end of the window), summed over the h days after t.
- **Features, all known at t:** trailing spending over 7, 28 and 56 days; the standard deviation of daily spending over 28 days and of the last 8 weekly totals; how many of each weekday fall in the horizon; days until the next expected payday and the number of expected paydays in the horizon (from the income series detected by t; none: 60 and 0); balance minus buffer at t; the month of t; h.
- **Normalization:** spending features, balance minus buffer and the target are divided by the persona's own trailing median weekly spending (the median of the last 8 weekly totals, at t; if that is 0, their mean; if that is 0 too, $1).
- **Model:** LightGBM, fixed settings, no tuning: 300 trees, learning rate 0.05, 15 leaves, at least 50 rows a leaf, 80 percent of rows and of features per tree, seed 0. An L1 objective for the middle, and quantile objectives at 10 and 90 percent for a band of D.
- **Training and evaluation:** pooled across the development personas, leave one persona out, with a 30-day gap: for a test persona and test origin t, the model is trained on the other 19 personas' rows whose origin is at least 30 days before t (so every target window used ends by t). Training rows come from every day from eight weeks after the history starts; test origins are the 40 backtest Mondays, kept only when at least 28 days of training origins exist before the gap (so about the last 31). The baseline is scored on the same test origins.
- **Compared with the weekday baseline on:** the mean absolute error of D at 7, 14 and 30 days; the balance error at 7, 14 and 30 days and the lowest-balance error of the single-path forecast with each spending path (same recurring payments and fees); and the coverage and width of the 10th-90th band of D at 7, 14 and 30 days (baseline band: 500 draws of the same weekly blocks the forecaster uses). Per archetype too.
- **Rule, fixed now:** LightGBM becomes a candidate only if its error of D at 30 days is at least 5 percent lower than the baseline's and its error at 7 days is not higher. Otherwise the baseline stays, and I say so. Development personas only; nothing merged.

### LightGBM spending model: result (leave one persona out, 30-day gap; experiment only)
640 test origins (the 32 backtest Mondays from 2026-01-26, 20 development personas), 22,296 training rows. Table in `docs/results/lightgbm_2026-10-10.md` (on the branch).
- Error of everyday spending D at 7 / 14 / 30 days: weekday baseline $95 / $140 / $229, LightGBM $116 / $170 / $275. LightGBM is worse at every horizon and in 7 of 8 archetypes at 30 days (DHOME is the exception: $230 against $236).
- Balance error of the single-path forecast: $511 / $1,017 / $1,672 against $523 / $1,025 / $1,688. Lowest balance: about the same ($357 / $822 against $356 / $819). Spending is a small part of the balance error; the big parts are the ones in the diagnosis.
- Band of D: the baseline's weekly draw covers 72 / 73 / 67 percent at 7 / 14 / 30 days, LightGBM's quantile models 63 / 60 / 63 percent.
- **By the rule fixed before training, the weekday baseline stays.** No setting was changed after seeing these numbers.
- Kept: the baseline. Only these two entries are on main; the code, its test and the results file stay on the branch `lightgbm-prototype`, which is kept for now and not merged.

## Proposals run (2026-10-11, branch `proposals`)

### 2026-10-11: The overnight run follows `docs/plans/overnight_2026-10-11.md`, with two additions of mine
- The run follows the plan step by step, with its "continue only if" checks and its stopping rule. Synthetic development personas only; no held-out persona, no final test. Results go to `docs/results/overnight_2026-10-11.md` (status after every step) and `docs/results/proposals_2026-10-11.md`.
- **My addition 1, for b) (repeated large outflows placed by default):** the gate also requires a precision of placements of at least 70 percent on the answer key, beside the recall of at least 50 percent in the plan. Why: a wrong detection placed by default, with no click, becomes a false alarm.
- **My addition 2, what a) is for:** a) is aimed at the student personas with large family transfers and tuition (IFAM, ITIGHT), not at my account. a) and b) are judged on the synthetic answer key, the report says which archetypes they help, and nothing is claimed about my account.

### 2026-10-11: Step 0: the random lump arrivals, rebuilt as a switch that is off by default (written before the code)
- The income prototype's branch is gone and its code was not brought over, so I write it again from its recorded design (Appendix A of the plan), as an option of the forecaster that is **off by default**. The app does not turn it on.
- **Lump:** a deposit the forecaster sets aside as a large deposit (`lump_rows`: in no series; at least $500 and 5 times the typical row of the 13 weeks before the forecast date; not paid straight back out within 14 days), posted in the 365 days up to the forecast date. Pass-throughs are not lumps. Tuition and other big payments stay set aside.
- **Rate:** the number of lumps divided by the days of the window (from the later of the first posted row and 365 days back, to the forecast date, both included). No history: rate 0.
- **Draw:** in each future, each forecast day gets a lump with probability equal to the rate (at most one a day), its size drawn at random from the observed lump sizes. Each future uses its own random stream named by the seed, the future and the word "lumps", so no other draw moves when lumps are switched on. Concretely: for each day in order, one uniform number decides whether a lump comes, and only if it does a second draw picks its size.
- **Where it counts:** lumps are irregular deposits: they are in the futures "with deposits", not in the median "without irregular deposits"; the what-if "no other money expected" turns them off. The single-path estimate does not change. Rule zero: only rows posted by the forecast date.
- **How it is switched:** `build_forecast(..., options=ForecastOptions(lump_arrivals=True))`; `SimulationInputs` gains `lump_rate` (0.0) and `lump_sizes` (empty).
- **Check before anything uses it:** with the switch off, the simulated output on three synthetic personas (v2-ITIGHT-01, v2-IFAM-02, v2-SAL-01; three origins each; 100 futures) must be identical to the forecaster before this change. I took the fingerprints of that output with the unchanged code before writing anything, and a test compares them. The prototype's tests are rebuilt too (Appendix A). If the identity or the rule-zero and own-stream tests fail, everything that needs arrivals (arms 1 to 5) stops.

### 2026-10-11: Step A: the 24-month persona set `v2L` (a new set of results, written before the code and before anything is generated)
- **What it is.** The same 30 persona specs as the frozen mix (archetypes, ranges, assignments, buffers, held-out list), ids `v2L-<ARCH>-<nn>`, history from 2024-10-01 to 2026-09-30 (24 months), generated with seed 42. Origins: the same 40 weekly Mondays, 2025-12-01 to 2026-08-31, so 14 to 23 months of history at an origin. Results on this set are never pooled with the 12-month set. **The frozen 12-month mix is not changed** (checked byte for byte, below).
- **Why the calendar has to be repeated, not only START moved.** The generator is written for one academic year: monthly schedules start in October 2025, wage breaks, exam dips and months away are 2025-26 dates, tuition falls due in January, May and September 2026, aid is paid on 2026 term dates. Moving START back alone would give a first year with no pay, no rent and no tuition. So in the long set **the same rules are applied to the year before**, with no new modelling:
  - recurring schedules start 12 months earlier (monthly ones on the same day of the month; weekly and biweekly ones 52 weeks earlier, so the pay weekday and phase stay);
  - wages: the anchor 52 weeks earlier; breaks, exam dips and months away repeated one year earlier (the same dates in 2024-25);
  - tuition: the Winter 2025, (Summer 2025) and Fall 2025 terms are added, with the same tuition amount as 2026 (the generator has no yearly rise), dates drawn by the same rule (Jan 15-25, May 15-25, Sep 7-17), the same installments, and the same funding rules (family deposit share and timing per term; ITIGHT's second transfer with probability 0.7; aid on the 2025 term dates and monthly bursary payments in the 2024-25 terms);
  - 1 to 3 more larger one-off purchases in the earlier year (the same rule, one year earlier).
- **Draws.** A long persona's parameters are drawn from the same stream as its 12-month namesake (so tuition, rent, pay and the coverage ratio are the same numbers), and every earlier-year draw comes from a separate stream named by the long id, so the 12-month draws are untouched. The day-by-day simulation (spending, quirks, fees, text) is seeded by the long id, so its rows differ from the 12-month persona's even in the overlapping year: it is not "the same persona with more rows". The coverage ratio is defined on a regular term month of the base year, as before.
- **Held out:** the same ten ids (`v2L-IFAM-04` and so on). The development-only guard refuses them and they are not generated for any evaluation here.
- **Known limitation, written down:** tuition amounts do not rise from year to year in the generator, while real tuition does; a payee-based rule that uses the latest amount is therefore easier here than in life. Fall 2024 tuition (September 2024) is before the window, so the first tuition in the history is Winter 2025.
- **Acceptance checks** (as for the frozen mix; `python -m eval.persona_report --check --set long`, generated balances only, development personas only), reported for each development persona: lowest balance, closing balance, and origins starting below the buffer. The same checks 1 to 3 as for the frozen mix are printed (IFAM at most 3 origins below the buffer; ITIGHT dips to its buffer plus $300 or lower and never below -$3,000; DHOME, DRENT, TIGHT, FREE at most 20 origins below). **Nothing is adjusted whatever comes out:** a failing persona is a recorded limitation.
- **Continue only if** the 12-month personas are unchanged (the export and answer key of every development persona hash to what they were before this change; the hashes were taken first) and every development persona of the long set generates and reconciles (importer PASS).
- **Stop line b)** (write up the table, the 12-month set stays the only set) if: more than half of the development personas that pay tuition from chequing have fewer than two tuition payments from one payee posted on or before the last origin (2026-08-31); or a development persona's closing balance (2026-09-30) is below -$20,000 (the plan's proposal). I read "ends below" as the closing balance and report the lowest balance beside it.
