### 2026-10-19: Stable series identity (week 5, item 1): what I chose and what lost
My decisions about a recurring series (confirm, reject, end, rename, category, edit, rent rule, "continues") were stored against the series key, and a card-cluster key ends in the median amount of its purchases, so it moves. A decision then stopped applying, or a confirmed habit was counted twice (2026-10-12). Now decisions are stored against a stable id.
- **The key stays as it is.** The forecast draws its random numbers from the key; changing it would change every simulated future and make the saved futures and the experiment 3 records stale. The id is separate. With no decisions nothing changes: raw detection is identical, and a persona re-run on the new code equals the experiment 3 records (40 of 40 origins, on a 12-month and a 24-month persona).
- **Won: an id plus a table of the payments remembered with it** (`series_members`). A series is found again by the payments it still shares with its remembered ones, so the id survives a moving median, new payments, older payments added by a later import, and the oldest payment dropping out. Tested.
- **Lost: an id derived only from the oldest payment's fingerprint.** It broke both of my own cases (older payments added later; the oldest payment dropping out), so I built the table instead.
- **A price change.** A new cluster carries on an older one of the same merchant and cadence that stopped appearing (starts after its last payment, within two periods), and the two are shown as one series with one history. Only for a series I have decided something about. Never from one I ended or rejected: a resubscription starts new.
- **One function writes decisions, always by id.** A test fails if any code writes an old key. Old keys are still read, so a database not yet migrated keeps working.
- **Migration** (`python -m lookahead.identity`): report, dry run (counts only, writes nothing), apply (a verified byte copy first, one transaction, a check that no decision is lost, rolled back otherwise). Decisions that name no series today are kept, never deleted, and re-attached only with `--reattach`, only when exactly one series of that family exists.
- **Versions.** I did not bump `LOGIC_VERSION` (it would mark 974 saved synthetic and Berka records stale for no change). A new `REPLAY_VERSION` (4) rebuilds the page cache and the Time Machine steps, which do depend on decisions.
- **Can this change my real forecast? Yes.** A confirmed habit that was counted twice is now counted once, and a decision that stopped applying can apply again after the migration. The apply output prints counts of series whose forecast contribution changed.

### 2026-10-20: Stable series identity: a leftover of a habit folds into it (found by my dry run)
My dry run on my real database showed 5 series before and 6 after. One habit had been joined by a "continue" link I made, with a confirmation on each side, and after the move it came back as two series (the older one inactive, so the forecast did not change). My first fix handled only a price change, and my test did not use a continue link.
- **The rule now:** a detected cluster that shares remembered payments with the series that holds an id folds into that series, whatever joined them (a continue link, a price change or a confirmed snapshot). The newest cluster survives and keeps the id my decisions name.
- **Genuinely ambiguous, left separate on purpose (no match is better than a doubtful match):** a cluster that shares payments with two or more other series; clusters paid in the same weeks (two real habits at one merchant); a snapshot whose payments sit in two or more series. An ended or rejected series is still never carried on, and a resubscription starts a new id.
- **The collision count** is now per series that two or more old keys land on (it counted decisions before). **`dry-run --explain`** prints the collision's kind, the series by kind, the extra series' decision actions and how many series still share payments. Counts and kinds only.
- **Result on my real database:** 5 series before and 5 after, 11 of 11 decisions applying, nothing added, removed or changed in the forecast, 0 series still sharing payments. The full suite found 3 tests that still wrote old keys; I fixed the tests, not the rule.

### 2026-10-20: "Looks right" proposals for recurring settings (week 5, item 2): the plan and the adopt rule, written before any proposer or planter code (branch `proposals-v2`)
Not run, nothing built when this is written. It follows my 2026-10-06 direction: the app proposes what I used to type by hand, prefilled from history, and nothing is applied without a click. These are **not** the lump-income and repeated-large-payment proposals of the old `proposals` branch (retired or left off on 2026-10-12); that branch is not touched.

**What is proposed, in this order.** (1) *Rent* sent by e-Transfer: the allowed amounts (clusters of amounts; the latest sizes are the ones used going forward) and the window of days of the month that covers most past payments. (2) *Link*: one subscription under two merchant names, proposed as a "continues" link. (3) *Possible subscription*: a monthly charge seen exactly twice, shown as "possible", lower confidence. A proposal is made for a series only if **I have made no decision about it** (not confirmed, rent rule, linked, ended, rejected or hand-added). "Looks right" writes the real recurring decision through `recurring_store.decide`, by stable id; "not this" is a dated row in `proposal_decisions`. Rule zero: a proposal on date *t* uses only rows posted on or before *t* and decisions made by *t*.

**The planting spec (a).** The existing persona sets stay byte-identical and the held-out personas are never read. A planter writes *copies* of the 20 development personas of the 12-month set and the 20 of the 24-month set (40 in all) to their own folder, with an answer key of what it planted. Seed 2026; one random stream per persona, named by (seed, persona id). Events, each decided independently per persona:
- **R1, late rent** (personas that pay rent by e-Transfer): with probability 0.6, from month 4 on, each rent payment is sent late with probability 0.4, by 2 to 8 days (uniform) after its usual day, with the usual business-day shift.
- **R2, rent increase** (the same personas): with probability 0.5, in a month drawn uniformly from 5 to 9, the rent rises by 5 to 12 percent, rounded to $5, for all later payments.
- **L1, renamed subscription** (personas with at least one subscription): with probability 0.5 one of them is picked; in a month drawn uniformly from 4 to 9 its text changes to a different made-up merchant name (not a prefix of the old one); amount and cadence unchanged, no payment skipped or doubled.
- **P1, new subscription:** with probability 0.5 a new monthly subscription (made-up name, whole dollars from $5 to $30) whose first charge is in month 10 or 11, so that it has exactly two charges at some origins.
- **Noise:** none beyond the above; amounts are exact, the rest of each persona is as generated.
- **Any later change to the planter (an event, a rate, a range, the seed) counts as a variant** (g).

**What counts as a match.** A proposal *matches* a planted event if it is about the right series and its content is right: rent: the allowed amounts include every size paid in the event and the window covers at least 80 percent of the planted send days; link: it joins exactly the old and the new name; possible subscription: it is the planted subscription. **Precision** (per kind) = matching proposals shown at any origin of a persona / all proposals of that kind shown there (planted or not). **Recall** (per kind) = planted events matched by a proposal shown within 60 days of the event becoming observable (rent: three payments at the new setting; link: two payments under the new name; possible subscription: its second charge). **n = the number of planted events of the kind, always reported.**

**Adopt rule, per kind (all must hold; a tie or a miss stays off).**
- **(b)** Precision at least 80 percent **and** recall at least 60 percent, with **at least 20 planted events** of that kind. A kind with fewer than 20 is reported with its n and **cannot pass**.
- **(c)** "One click" (accept every proposal that is valid at the origin, using only data up to it) is **not worse than zero confirmations by more than 1 percent on the 30-day error for any archetype** (20 development personas per set, 40 weekly origins, 500 futures, seed 42, the sets never pooled).
- **(d)** The zero-confirmation forecasts are **unchanged from today's default**: with the proposers present and nothing clicked, the saved default's p10, p50, p90, dip chance, median lowest balance and CRPS equal the experiment 3 records at 800 of 800 origins on each set (proposers change nothing unless I click).
- **(e)** A precision **above 95 percent** triggers a leakage check (does any proposer read a row posted after the origin, or the answer key) before anything is claimed.
- **(f) My real account can only veto.** A counts-only script I run myself prints, as yes/no counts with no amounts, dates or names, whether the proposers would recover my existing hand entries (the rent rule, the continue link, the hand-added series). A "no" for a kind stops its adoption.
- **(g) Every variant tried is counted in the report** (a change to a proposer or to the planter after the first scoring run is a variant); real and synthetic results stay in separate columns. Planted events are designed by me, so a score on them is development, not evaluation.

**Order of work and what is not done here.** Steps 1 to 5 (the three proposers, apply and dismiss, the Recurring page panel) are built first, with ordinary tests, before the planter. Step 6 (the planter and its answer key) and step 7 (the scoring script) come after this entry; no scoring is run on this laptop while I work.

### 2026-10-20: "Looks right" proposals: I narrowed the guard before any scoring (follow-up to the entry above)
The entry above says a proposal is made for a series only if I have made no decision about it. That was too broad: one click on "Looks right, confirm all" would have stopped every rent and link proposal for good, and confirming or renaming a series says nothing about whether its rent window or a link is right. So the guard now depends on the kind of proposal:
- **Rent:** blocked by a rent rule already on the series, by "It ended" or "Not recurring" (the latest of confirm, reject and end counts), or by "Not this". Confirming, renaming and a category do not block it.
- **Link:** blocked by an existing link involving either series, by an end or a reject on either series, or by "Not this". Confirming, renaming and a category do not block it.
- **Possible subscription:** blocked by any decision on it (a name I added, or payments that already belong to a series), or by "Not this".
- **A series I added by hand never gets a proposal.**
I made this change before the planter was written and before any scoring run, so nothing scored is affected; the adopt rule (b) to (g) is unchanged. Any change to a proposer after the first scoring run still counts as a variant. Tests: a confirmed series with no rent rule still gets a rent proposal, a renamed series still gets a link proposal, "confirm all" hides nothing, and a live call uses every decision (including a click after the data's last day) while a replay as of an earlier date ignores later ones.

### 2026-10-20: A rent rule keeps the rent going, and rent that stopped is not proposed (settled before the planter or any scoring)
I wrote a test for the open risk before building anything else: after a rent increase I accept a rent proposal that covers the old and the new amount, and the forecast must place the next rent. **It failed**: the schedule skips any series detection calls inactive, even one with a rent rule, so a rule on the old-amount series (inactive once the old amount stops and the new one is not yet a series) placed nothing. Two more cases failed with it: the proposal was attached to the stale old-amount series when a live one existed, and a rent that stopped months ago was still proposed.
- **Fix, replay:** a series with a rent rule is active unless I ended it ("It ended"); "Not recurring" still wins; confirming again after an end puts it back. Replay version 5, so saved pages and Time Machine steps rebuild. With no decisions nothing changes, so the zero-confirmation forecasts and the saved futures are untouched.
- **Fix, proposer:** a rent proposal goes to the live series when there is one, and is not made when the latest payment is more than 50 days before the data ends.
- Made before the planter and before any scoring run, so it is not a variant of anything scored.

The existing sets are byte-identical: the combined hash of the 40 regenerated development exports and of the 6 stored synthetic files is the same before and after. Nothing has been scored, so this is a change of the spec, not of a result. The planter is now **version 2**; the first version (rates 0.6, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5 and P1 in month 10 or 11) generated copies that were never scored.
- **Why.** Generating the first copies showed rent n = 18, link n = 9, possible n = 25, and only 10 of the 25 possible-subscription events observable at an origin (a first charge in August has no second charge before the last origin, 31 August). Under the adopt rule as written no kind could pass.

I tried 0.8, 0.7, 0.8, 0.8 first: it gave rent 25, link 21, possible 35, so rent and link did not reach about 30. **Link is capped:** one rename per persona, and only 24 of the 40 personas have a subscription that qualifies, so 24 is the most the kind can have; L1 1.0 reaches it. Nothing else in the spec changed (the ranges, the 2 to 8 days, 5 to 12 percent, $5 to $30, the seed 2026, the month numbering from October 2025).

All of them are observable by the last origin, so 0 are excluded from recall. Every kind has at least 20. The existing sets are byte-identical: the combined hash of the 40 regenerated development exports is the same before and after (`269582dafe35...`), and so is that of the 6 stored synthetic files (`53c22d966728...`).
- **Recall is now counted only over planted events whose evidence is complete by the last origin**, as the planter's answer key defines it (`observable_from` on or before 31 August 2026), never by what a proposer showed. The number excluded as not yet observable is reported per kind. The other parts of rule (b) are unchanged: precision at least 80 percent, recall at least 60 percent, and at least 20 planted events of the kind (planted events, not only counted ones).
- **The card-pair change to the possible-subscription proposer (`a968bfc`)** was made after the planter was written and before any scoring: a card pair is proposed when the two amounts are exactly equal and a calendar month apart, because real subscriptions are card payments and the first version wrongly excluded them. **Precision on the planted card pairs is likely flattering**, because the proposer matches exactly what the planter plants. The report repeats this every time.
- **Variants.** No variant has been scored yet. The first scored run uses planter version 2; the ledger counts it as variant 1, and any later change to the planter or to a proposer counts as another.
- **Also added, not part of the adopt rule:** `eval.proposals_recover` (the veto script (f)) was checked to hide my rent rules, links and hand-added series from its in-memory copy before asking whether the proposers would recover them, and a test now pins that; and `eval.rent_rule_origins`, which counts, with no names, at how many past origins the rent fix (`3c5c12e`) could change my real forecast (those where detection alone calls a series with a rent rule inactive and I had not ended it).

### 2026-10-20: My real-account veto run: no unit or sign bug in the rent proposer; the veto script missed the migration's merge (the proposers are unchanged)
Running the veto script on my real account gave "no" for all three kinds. What the code says: **rent** `amount_too_small` is not a sign or unit bug (the importer stores signed integer cents and the proposer compares the size with 10,000 cents; ordinary rent imported through the real importer is proposed, tested). The floor is applied to the median of **every** outgoing e-Transfer to the recipient, and e-Transfers with no printed recipient are one group, so many small e-Transfers can pull rent under $100: a design question for me, not changed. **Link** `no_candidate_series` was partly the script's fault: the migration remembered both names' payments under one id, which merged them again with the link hidden; the script now undoes that merge in its copy (tested with the real migration). **Hand-added** `amounts_differ` comes from the possible-subscription proposer, whose two payments must be equal to within 1 percent (exactly equal if paid by card); not changed. `--explain` now also prints counts-only detail lines (the rent recipient's payment counts; how many series detection alone finds for a link). Any change to a proposer prompted by this is recorded here as prompted by my real account and counted as a variant.

### 2026-10-21: Two proposer changes and a planter change, all prompted by my real-account veto run (variants, and the rerun after them is not an independent check)
**Where they come from.** I ran the veto script on my real account (counts only). It said no to all three kinds at first; after the script's own fixes, rent and hand-added still said no, and the link entry said yes once the script stopped being fooled by the migration. So these changes are **prompted by my real account**, not by the planted development personas.
**What I decided (the code comes after this entry):**
1. **Rent pooling.** Within any recipient group, named or unnamed, the rent proposal is judged on a **cluster** of payments that are similar in amount, roughly monthly and at or above the $100 floor, instead of the median of the whole group. Small e-Transfers to the same recipient or to none must not pull rent under the floor; a group of only small transfers still gets no proposal; occasional payments to a friend are not rent.
2. **Possible-subscription tolerance.** The two payments may differ by up to **5 percent** of the larger amount (it was 1 percent), for card pairs as well as others; card pairs keep the calendar-month gap rule. Two one-off purchases at one merchant a month apart within 5 percent are still only "possible" (low confidence) and are never confirmed by themselves.
3. **Planter additions (planter version 3).** Rent paid by unnamed e-Transfer among many small unnamed e-Transfers, and possible-subscription pairs with currency-style differences of 1 to 4 percent, at rates that keep every kind at 20 planted events or more. Nothing else in the planter changes; the existing persona sets stay byte-identical.
**What this means for the evidence.**
- **Each is a variant** and goes in the variant ledger (the two proposer changes and the planter change are three entries), together with the first scored run, which is counted after them.
- **The planter change is a planter change** under the rule of 2026-10-20: any later change to the planter counts as a variant.
- **The veto rerun after these changes is not an independent check.** I chose the changes because of what the real account showed, so passing it afterwards is expected; it shows the changes work on my case, not that they would have worked blind. The report and the veto script say so every time.
- Real and synthetic results stay in separate columns; the planted scores remain development, not evaluation.
**Not changed:** every other proposer rule (the guards, the freshness limits, the link rules, the 5 percent link amount tolerance, the card-month gap) and the adopt rule.

### 2026-10-22: When a rent proposal "recovers" my rent rule: decided on product grounds, prompted by my real account (a change of the veto, not of the proposers)
After the three changes of 2026-10-21 my veto rerun said rent **no, amounts_not_equal, although a proposal exists** (link yes, hand-added yes). The old test asked for the same *set* of allowed amounts, which is not what the forecast uses: it places **only the larger allowed amount** (the cautious reading) and uses the window to predict the date, while payments are matched by amount whatever the day. So I decide now, before looking at which part differs, that a rent proposal **recovers** my rent rule if both hold:
- **(a) the amount it would use going forward equals mine**: the larger allowed amount of the proposal equals the larger allowed amount of my rule;
- **(b) its window covers the days of my past rent payments that fall inside my rule's window**: every one of those days; if none fall inside my window there is nothing to cover.
Missing or extra older allowed amounts, and a wider or narrower window, are **reported but do not veto**. `--explain` prints counts only for rent: forward amount same (yes or no), allowed amounts missing and extra, payment days covered (N of M).
This is prompted by my real account and the veto stays a veto: a "no" for a kind still stops that kind. The proposers are not changed, so this is not a proposer variant; it changes what the veto asks, and a veto rerun under the new definition is still not an independent check (the definition was chosen after the old one failed on my account).

### 2026-10-22: Bug fix (rent proposer), prompted by my real-account veto: the newest amount was lost after a small rise
The spec (81cb152: "the latest sizes are the ones used going forward") was broken: amounts within 3 percent (or $5) of one another are one size, and a size was allowed only at its **median**, so after a small rise the proposal could stay on the old amount and miss the new one. A size is now allowed at its median **and at its latest amount**; the window rule (built from every payment of the rent cluster, 80 percent coverage, at most 14 days), the veto definition and everything else are unchanged. Tests on made-up data (a rise within 3 percent, a rise within $5, a rise that is a size of its own, a constant amount). Logged on the variant ledger as a bug fix.

### 2026-10-22: The rent window covers every send day when it can (a variant, prompted by my real account; the veto definition is unchanged)
My veto rerun after the bug fix still said rent **no** (`window_misses_payment_days`). The proposer's window covers only 80 percent of a cluster's send days by design, and the veto asks for every day of mine inside my window. I decided to change the proposer, not the veto:
- **New window rule.** The window is the shortest span of days of the month that covers **all** the send days of the rent cluster, at most 14 days wide, wrapping a month end allowed. If no span of 14 days or less covers all of them, it falls back to the current rule: the shortest span covering at least 80 percent, with the same tie-breaks.
- **The veto definition stays exactly as committed in 3861996** (the same forward amount; the window covers the days of my past payments inside my window; the rest only reported). Nothing else in the proposers changes.
- **It is a variant** and goes on the variant ledger, prompted by my real account. **The veto rerun after it is not an independent check:** I chose the rule because of what that account showed, so a pass shows the change works on my case, not that it would have worked blind. The report and the veto script keep saying so.
- A cost I accept: one late payment now stretches the window, so the window can be wider than the days I usually pay; the 14-day limit and the fallback bound that.

### 2026-10-22: Rent is vetoed by my account in this round (under the 3861996 definition); no more proposer changes for my account
My final veto rerun (not independent, after every change prompted by my account) gave: **rent no, window_misses_payment_days**; link yes; hand-added yes. For rent the cluster is the right one; what differs is the window: the 80 percent fallback leaves out a day inside my own window, and the veto requires every one of them.
- **I accept the veto for rent.** Under the definition committed in 3861996 (the same forward amount; the window covers every day of my past payments inside my window) the proposer does not recover my rent rule, and I will not change the proposers again for my account in this round.
- **A "most days within 14" window would not clearly help:** on my account it leaves out the same day as the 80 percent fallback.
- Link (yes) and hand-added (yes) pass the veto. The rent kind stays marked **"vetoed by my account"** in every report, whatever part (b) shows, and it is not adopted.
- This is a counts-only reading of my real account; real and synthetic results stay in separate columns, and the veto remains not independent of the changes made after it.

### 2026-10-22: Scoring runs in two parts, and the proposers and planter are frozen once part (b) has run
Scoring the "Looks right" proposals has two parts, as the adopt rule of 2026-10-20 says. **Part (b)**, the precision and recall of each kind on the planted development copies, runs **now on this laptop** (one job, stopping before memory use passes 92 percent). **Part (c)**, one click against zero confirmations on the 30-day error, is heavy and runs **later on the MacBook**. **Nothing is adopted until both are done**, and the real account can still veto (rent already is: see the entry above).
**The proposers and the planter are frozen once part (b) has run.** Part (b) runs on planter version 3 and the proposers as committed when it starts; any change to a proposer or to the planter after that is **a new variant** on the ledger (`docs/results/proposals_v2_ledger.json`), and part (b) would have to be run again for it. Part (b) is not an independent check of the changes I made after my real-account veto (they are listed on the ledger and the report says so), and its precision on planted card pairs is likely flattering (the report says that too). The report is saved under `docs/results/` with a run record.

### 2026-10-23: Memory stop line for part (b) raised from 92 to 95 percent (an operating setting)
I raised the memory stop line from 92% to 95% because this 8 GB laptop sits at 91 to 93% with only VS Code and one browser tab open, and the first resume stopped at 93% before scoring a persona. It is an operating setting only: it changes nothing about the proposers, the planter, the rule or what is scored, and each persona is saved as it finishes, so a stop at the new line still loses nothing.

### 2026-10-23: Part (b) result: fails as written for rent and possible; link is pending the leakage check; nothing is adopted
Part (b) ran on the 40 planted development personas (run part-b-20261022-132930, planter version 3, proposers as at d668df5, both sets together, report and run record in `docs/results/`). **Rent fails** (precision 44 percent, recall 43 percent) and **possible subscription fails** (precision 30 percent; recall 100 percent). **Link** has 24 of 24 on both precision and recall, which is above the 95 percent flag, so **it stays pending the leakage check of rule (e)**. **Rent is also vetoed by my account** (the entry of 2026-10-22), whatever (b) shows.
- **The scorer has two limits that I have now seen in the results.** (1) A real recurring series that the planter did not plant counts as wrong: all 132 unmatched possible proposals are such series (with three or more payments in the full history), most of them caught in the first weeks of the 12-month set. (2) Each rent proposal is scored only on the first version it had, because its key stays the same while its rule changes, and it is judged against the event's final state. These limits explain a large part of the gaps between the two sets (possible precision 18 versus 71 percent; rent recall 67 versus 20 percent). They are not a finding that the proposers are fine.
- **I am not rescoring now.** Any corrected scorer would be **a new counted variant, defined and committed before it runs**, on the ledger like the others. Nothing here changes the proposers, the planter, the rule or the scorer.
- **Nothing is adopted.** Part (c) has not been run, and adoption needs (b) and (c) both.

### 2026-10-23: Recurring alerts (week 5, item 3): what I chose, before any code (branch `recurring-alerts`, from main)
A plain product feature: ordinary tests on made-up data, no pre-registered rule, no scoring. The forecaster is not touched (zero-confirmation forecasts are unchanged) and the new code imports neither it nor the proposers.
- **Four alerts, in forecast words** ("expected payment not seen yet", "amount looks different", "may have stopped", "new recurring payment"). Not seen, amount different and stopped are raised only for series I confirmed or added by hand, so a wrong detection cannot nag me. "New recurring payment" is the one kind that covers unconfirmed series: a detected series I have decided nothing about that was not there at the last look.
- **Not seen:** the due date plus 3 business days of grace (the detector's own tolerance); a series with a rent rule waits until its window has ended plus the same grace. **Amount different:** the latest payment is outside the series' range, or a fixed amount changed. **Stopped:** the series was active at the last look, is not now, and I did not end it.
- **The rent guard:** a series with a rent rule never raises "may have stopped"; only I end rent. Done on main by that check alone. I did not take the `proposals-v2` change that keeps a rent-rule series active, so the two branches do not clash on `REPLAY_VERSION`.
- **Episodes and dismissal copy the shortfall alert's shape, not its code.** `alerts.py` imports the forecaster and its tables hold a low and a chance, so the new alerts get their own module and tables (episode per stable series id and kind; events never edited). A dismissed episode speaks again only if it gets materially worse in kind: not seen, when a second due date also passes unpaid; amount, when it is higher than at the dismissal by 10 percent and $10; new recurring and stopped never re-alert (confirming, rejecting or a new payment resolves them). Better news stays quiet. Looking again at the same numbers adds nothing.
- **Provisional constants:** 10 percent and $10 for an amount change, 3 business days of grace. Picked to be noticeable, not measured.
- **The previous look: changed after measuring.** My first choice was to store only the data end date of the last look and recompute the earlier series with `series_as_of` at that date. Measured on made-up data (one persona, this laptop, memory near 90 percent): about 0.5 s for 12 months (784 rows), but about 4.5 s for 24 months (1,560 rows). That is over the 2 s I set, so I switched, as I had said I would: the state row now holds the last look's data end date and a map from each series' stable id to whether it was active. Stopped and new are compared against that map. It moves forward only when the data date does, and the first look on a database only sets the baseline (nothing is "new" or "stopped" then). Lost: the recompute (rule-zero clean without storing anything, but too slow at two years of history).
- **Known limit.** A detected series counts only payments within its own tolerance of a due date. A payment much later than that (say 11 days after a monthly bill) is not part of the series, so "not seen yet" stays until the detector sees a pattern again. A series I added by hand matches its payments by name on any day, and a series with a rent rule counts any day, so for those a late payment closes the alert.
- **When it runs:** when the Recurring page opens and after an import, through the same function. Recurring page only (no Home count). Alerts are not replayed in the Time Machine.

### 2026-11-02: Scenario calculator ("what if I spend X a day"): what I chose, before any code
A plain product feature (branch `scenario`, from main): ordinary tests on made-up data, no adopt rule, no scoring.
- **A calculator, not a forecast.** `lookahead/scenario.py` adds up exactly what is entered: a starting balance, an end date (31 December of this year by default), recurring flows, income, a daily spend, one-off items and an optional buffer, all in integer cents. It gives a balance for every day to the end date, the end balance and the first day below the buffer. No randomness and no band. It imports nothing from the forecaster or the proposers (a test checks it). Recurring dates use the detector's own cadence rules (`lookahead.recurring.expected_dates`, with the same weekend and holiday shifts), not a second copy.
- **Prefilled from the database** by a small adapter: the latest balance; my confirmed and hand-added series; a series with a rent rule as its latest allowed amount (the amount of my latest rent payment if it is allowed, otherwise the larger one) on its usual day (the most common day of the month of its payments); income as the average per month over the last 90 days on its usual day, editable; the daily spend as the median day over the last 90 days of money out that is not in a recurring series, not rent, not a transfer and not a netted pair. What cannot be estimated starts at zero and the page says so.
- **Three readings I chose:** income counts only income rows that are not already in a confirmed or hand-added series, so a payroll series is not counted twice; the median is over every day of the window, days with no spending included; with less than 30 days of history the daily spend is not estimated.
- **The page** compares "as things are now" (the prefilled values) with "my scenario", with the buffer line and the first day below it, the headline "You'd have $X on <date>", and under the chart: "This assumes exactly what you entered. It's not a forecast."
- **Lost: reusing the forecast's what-if** (`lookahead/forecast/scenario.py`). It reruns the random futures and answers a different question; this one has to be simple and exact.
- **Changed after my real-data check (2026-11-02): rent was right, income and the daily spend were not.**
  - **Income** is now all money in over the last 90 days, scaled to a month, less what is not income: transfers between my own accounts (the flow typing's "transfer", in both directions), refunds, refunded pairs, and money already in a confirmed or hand-added series. **Big one-off deposits are left out too (2026-11-02, option A):** I moved the one-time money rule out of the forecaster unchanged (its own entry), and the prefill leaves out the deposits the forecaster sets aside (large deposits and pass-throughs). The basis note says how many deposits were counted and how many one-off deposits were left out.
  - **Income timing:** by default spread evenly through the month (each calendar month adds up to the monthly amount exactly, in whole cents), or on a set day I can change. The choice applies to both lines, since it describes how money arrives rather than a what-if.
  - **Top-ups from savings per month:** a separate field, zero by default, counted as money in and arriving like income.
  - **The daily spend is the average day, not the median day:** the total of the money out that counts, divided by the days of the window, days with no spending included. The median of a month with a few big shopping days is close to zero, which understates spending. Same exclusions as before.
  - **Each estimate shows its basis** under its field (how many deposits or payments over how many days, and what was left out).

### 2026-11-10: Scenario calculator v2: chequing and savings together (after my second real-data check)
My second check on my real data failed: the year-end balance looked wrong, and I could not see the basis notes. The spec is `docs/specs/2026-11-10-scenario-v2.md`; what I chose:
- **Chequing and savings together by default.** The starting balance is my latest chequing balance plus a savings balance I type in (savings is not imported). Transfers between my own accounts cancel out: they were already neither income nor spending, and a confirmed recurring series of them is now left out of the combined view (it stays in the chequing-only view). Without a savings balance the page says it shows chequing only, and only then shows "Top-ups from savings".
- **What cancels out is only the bank's own-account transfer** (the "customer transfer" rows), unless I typed the row as something else. A bill payment to my own card or credit line does not cancel: that money leaves chequing and savings for good, so a confirmed series of them stays counted (found by the review before my check).
- **The daily spend counts everything not counted elsewhere (2026-11-10, my decision).** Until now it left out every row typed as a transfer, so card payments outside a series, e-Transfers to other people and withdrawals were missing, which is wider than "transfers between my own accounts" in the entry above. Now only these are left out, because they are counted elsewhere: rent (a series with a rent rule, which has its own field), confirmed or hand-added recurring series, and transfers between my own accounts; refunded pairs stay out because they net to zero. The payments of a series that is only detected now count as spending, since the page does not list it. Two consequences, so nothing is counted nowhere or carried on by mistake: rent with a rule fills the rent field even when its series is not confirmed yet (its payments are left out of the spend), and a confirmed series that has ended stays out of the spend (one that is not rent is not listed, so it is not carried on; an ended series with a rent rule is still listed, see the open point below).
- **Open (my decision):** (1) an ended rent series with a rule (after a move, say) is still listed beside the current rent and carried on until I switch it off on the page; (2) income still leaves out only active confirmed series, so the deposits of an ended confirmed pay series in the last 90 days count in "income per month". The spend note says what was counted and what was left out. Lost: keeping the old rule and only saying so in the note (it made the daily spend too low for anyone who pays a card or other people from chequing).
- **Counted twice, chequing-only view:** when a confirmed top-up from savings is listed and the top-ups field is not zero, the page shows a short warning that the same money may be counted twice (I chose a warning over hiding the field). A confirmed top-up from savings is not an income series either. Known limits: an own-account transfer to an account outside the two (a TFSA, a credit line) would also cancel, and there is one savings balance per database, not per account.
- **The savings balance is remembered** in the app settings of my local database with the day I entered it, and a Clear button forgets it.
- **Income:** transfers from other people count, as incoming e-Transfers already did; a big one-off deposit is left out of the average by the shared one-time money rule; an expected one is a dated one-off "money in".
- **The notes are never blank:** when nothing could be estimated they say why. On the old version the notes didn't show on my data; v2 always shows them.
- **The buffer and its marker apply to the balance the chart shows** (the combined total, or chequing alone).
- **The default end date is 90 days after my last data date** (it was December 31, which grows less useful through the year).
- **Lost:** leaving the calculator chequing-only and asking me to type a monthly top-up (it depends on a number I don't know in advance, and a wrong one moves the whole line); importing savings (not in v1).

### 2026-11-17: Scenario v2, the two open points decided
My decisions on the two open points of the scenario v2 entry above (2026-11-10):
- **An ended rent series is dropped when another rent series is still active** (after a move, say): it is neither the rent field nor a listed flow, and its payments stay out of the daily spend as rent. With no rent series active, the most recently paid one still fills the rent field, as before, and I can switch it off on the page. Lost: listing the old rent beside the new one until I switch it off (it carried a rent I no longer pay into the scenario).
- **An ended confirmed pay or income series is left out of income, same as spending:** its deposits in the last 90 days no longer count in "income per month", and it is not listed. If nothing else counts, the income note says no deposits were counted and income is left at zero for me to type. Lost: counting those deposits (an income that has ended would then still count).
Tests on made-up data for both, and for the case with no active rent series.

### 2026-11-17: Scenario v2, my third real-data check failed (not shipped)
What passed: the chequing-only note and the top-ups field show; one rent line, the old rent gone; the end date about 90 days out. What failed, and what was done:
- **The daily spend is far too high on my data.** The cause is not known yet. A counts-only check, `python -m lookahead.scenario_check --db PATH`, sorts every row of the page's window with the page's own rules (the same functions, so it cannot drift from the page) and prints row counts and percent shares only: what the spend rule did with each row (counted, rent, confirmed series, own-account transfer, refunded pair), the counted rows by flow-typing reason and by stored flow type and its source, how concentrated the counted total is, the counted rows in a detected-only series, the same for income, and how many series each view lists. It opens the database read-only and never saves series. I run it on my data; the fix waits on its counts. A suspect from reading the code: a row the bank types as an own-account transfer stops counting as one when a stored flow type (the categorizer's included) says something other than "transfer".
- **A dated one-off "money in" made no change:** reproduced on synthetic data. An item after the end date (or on or before the last day of data) was listed but skipped without a word, and a date a few months ahead can fall after the end date now that it is 90 days after the data. Now each such item says why it is not counted and how to include it (move the end date), and a warning counts them. Lost: moving the end date by itself to cover the latest item (it would change what I typed without telling me).
- **I could not find the counts in the notes:** they were mid-sentence in small grey captions. Each note is now a labelled block, "How the daily spend was estimated" and "How the income was estimated", with one line per count (days in the window, payments or deposits counted, and each thing left out), then the rule in words.
Tests on made-up data for each; the page and the check share one function per estimate.

### 2026-11-17: Scenario daily spend leaves out big one-off payments (after my diagnostic)
My counts-only check found the cause: big one-off payments were counted in the daily spend. What I chose:
- **The shared one-time money rule now applies to money out too:** a big one-off payment (at least $500 and at least 5 times the typical payment of the last 13 weeks), and money paid straight back out of a big deposit, is left out of the daily-spend average, counted in the note ("Left out, big one-off payments: N"), and can be added back as a dated one-off money out. `lookahead.one_time.big_payments` picks exactly the money out the forecaster sets aside when given the same series rows (a test checks they agree with no series rows; the scenario passes the rows of every series I have not rejected, detected ones included); the forecaster itself is unchanged. A payment of a series I have not rejected is not one (its series places it, as for deposits). One consequence: a single card payment of $500 or more, outside a series, among small purchases is now a big one-off payment too.
- **No single row may dominate the counted daily spend:** the check now warns when the largest counted row is more than 25% of the counted total, and tests hold the page to that line on the big-bill case and on the synthetic personas A, B and C (the demo personas, not held out).
- **The one-off money in:** I could not reproduce "no change" with an item well inside the window: on synthetic data in the chequing-only view, with a savings balance and with set-day timing, the item is listed and the end of "my scenario" moves by exactly its amount. The likely cause on my data is that the date I used was after the end date (90 days after the last day of data); the page now says so beside the item. To rule out a lost click, the one-off fields are now a form sent together with Add, an Add with no amount says so, and a line under the headline says how many one-offs are counted and their total.
Lost: leaving big bills in the average (one big bill can dominate the daily spend); a lower floor than the shared rule's $500 (two rules for the same idea).

### 2026-11-17: Scenario income: top-ups from savings no longer hide a one-off deposit (found in my nav-rework check)
My real-data check of `nav-rework` passed except income per month, which looked wrong; main showed the same, so the navigation was not the cause, and the prefill and the shared one-time money rule were unchanged since an earlier build where income looked right. Reproduced on synthetic data: the shared rule judges a deposit against the typical (median) money-in row of the last 13 weeks, and that can include transfers in from one's own savings. On one made-up account, twelve top-ups of $900 to $3,000 raised the typical row until an $8,000 one-off deposit was under 5 times it, so it counted as income ($2,858 a month instead of $152: the test `test_top_ups_from_savings_do_not_hide_a_lump_sum_from_the_one_off_rule`). Known limits: (1) an account whose pay is in no series and that has top-ups can now have fewer than 5 deposits, and then the rule judges a deposit against everything, as main already does for an account without top-ups, so a pay deposit can be taken for a one-off; (2) the same hiding could happen on the payment side with transfers out to savings; nothing I have seen points to either on my data, and my counts-only check shows both.

### 2026-11-02: "What changed since last time" (week 5, item 4): what it shows and when it moves on (before any code)
A plain product feature (branch `since-last-time`, from main): ordinary tests on made-up data, no adopt rule, no scoring. Everything is counted in code; nothing is written by an LLM.
- **What it shows.** A "Since last time" card on Home with the counts, linking to its own page with five sections: the balance change and how many rows are new; new merchants (first seen after the marker; one flagged for checking links to To check); recurring changes (the recurring alerts opened since the marker: new, may have stopped, amount looks different, not seen yet); category spending changes; and the alerts opened since the marker (shortfall and recurring), linking to their pages. Names are pseudonymized as on the other pages, and the words are "possible" and "estimate".
- **The marker** is per account: the data date I last acknowledged. **It moves only when I click "Got it".** On a database with no marker the page says there is nothing to compare yet and sets nothing until I click.
- **It is separate from the recurring alerts' last look.** Opening the page records a recurring-alerts look (the same function the Recurring page calls), which closes the gap I found: the import command records no look. Recording a look never moves the marker.
- **Category changes** compare money out by category since the marker with the same length of time just before it (transfers and netted pairs left out). A change counts only if it is **at least $25 and at least 20 percent**. Both thresholds are provisional, picked to be noticeable and not measured. If the earlier period starts before the data does, the comparison is not shown.
- **Changed after my real-data check (2026-11-02): new rows are the rows added after "Got it", whatever their posted date.** My October import stored more rows than the page counted: an export made during a day misses that day's later postings, and the next export brings them with the old date, so counting rows posted after the marker's date left them out (and any new merchant among them). "Got it" now also remembers the newest stored row and the balance shown; a marker set before this change counts by date until I click again.

### 2026-11-02: "What changed since last time" merged into main
I merged `since-last-time` into main (a fast-forward) after my own check on my real database with an October import, and after the full suite passed on it (2,265 tests). One change came after my check and is in what was merged: new rows are now the rows added after "Got it" rather than the rows posted after its date (my check found the page counting fewer rows than the import stored). My current marker predates that change, so it counts by date until I click "Got it" again.

### 2026-11-02: Exports in Windows-1252 are read too; a "?" already in the file stays (branch `import-encoding`)
After my October import some merchant names showed a "?" where an accented letter belongs. The importer read every export as strict UTF-8 and refused anything else (a test said so), and my October file imported without a format error. So it was valid UTF-8: **the "?" was already in the file the bank gave me**, and no reading setting can bring that letter back.
- **What changes anyway:** an export that is not valid UTF-8 is now read as Windows-1252 (the other encoding French text comes in), strictly, so a byte that belongs to neither is still refused and nothing is ever replaced by "?". The import prints which encoding each file was read in. The same rows in either encoding give the same fingerprints, so nothing is stored twice. Lost: refusing every non-UTF-8 file (simpler, but a Windows-1252 export would not import at all).
- **A counts-only check** (`python -m lookahead.ingest.textcheck`) counts, per export and per import day of the stored rows, the rows with accented letters, with a "?" touching a letter, and with the Unicode replacement character. It prints counts only and changes nothing; it is how I confirm where the "?" come from.
- **No stored row is changed.** A repair would need an export of the same days that still has the letters, matched on date, amount, balance and description; it changes fingerprints, which other tables use as keys, so it would be its own step with a backup, a dry run with counts, and tests.

### 2026-11-02: The one-time money rule moves out of the forecaster, unchanged (so the scenario calculator can use it)
I moved the rule that finds big one-time money and pass-throughs from `lookahead/forecast/spending.py` to `lookahead/one_time.py`, which imports nothing from the forecaster; the forecaster imports it back (the old names still work from `spending`). A pure move: the moved functions and constants are identical to the old ones (compared as parsed code), and the forecaster's output did not change. **The proof:** I ran the full forecaster (500 futures, every path kept) for all 40 synthetic development personas (both sets) at every fourth origin, 400 origins, on main before the move and on the move's commit, and wrote each origin's record (without its version field, which is the commit) with hashes of the paths and of everything the forecast set aside. The two files are byte-identical; 109 of the 400 origins had big one-time rows set aside, so the rule was exercised. The linter had dropped one re-exported name that eval scripts import (the full suite caught it); after putting it back I ran the comparison again, byte-identical again, and the full suite passed. `eval.deposit_rule` now changes the cutoff constants where they live.

### 2026-11-02: Experiment 4: how I read the rule, fixed before any scoring
Written while the scoring code was being built and before any experiment 4 number was computed. The rule of 2026-10-17 (ed7f16d) is not changed; these are my readings of its words where they were ambiguous.
- **The lag.** The written inequality is the rule: at origin *t* only origins *t'* with *t' <= t - 35 days* count. Origins are weekly, so with the minimum of 8 an account gets its own weight at **28 of 40** origins. "27 of 40" in the rule was a miscount.
- **Condition 11.** On each synthetic set, "the mean CRPS" is the mean over archetypes of each archetype's CRPS averaged over days 1 to 30, every origin. On Berka it is the day-30 CRPS with accounts weighted equally (the measure of condition 8). "Falls" means strictly lower than the default.
- **Condition 6 when no threshold meets the bar.** The threshold is chosen by the same rule as before (`thresholds_raw.choose`: at least 70 percent precision and the false-alert cap, then the highest recall). A method with no threshold meeting both catches 0 dips: the condition fails if only the variant has none, and passes with a written note if both have none.
- **The eligible-origins stop.** On Berka I report the share of origins that get a learned weight for (ii) and (iii) separately, and I stop if either is below half. An origin gets a learned weight when it meets that variant's minimums, whatever weight comes out.
- **Condition 3.** "At least half of its origins at w < 1" counts the archetype's ordinary origins only (not already below the line), the ones its error and bias are measured on.
- **Four readings I took as clear, now confirmed.** The median *m(h)* is the forecast's p50 (the simulator's nearest-rank percentile, as saved). The global weight averages loss(w) / loss(1) over every resolved origin of the other accounts, each origin counted once; on synthetic the other accounts are the personas of the other archetypes in the same set, and "at least 5 other accounts" means 5 with at least one resolved origin. The dip measures (error, bias, coverage, Brier, dips caught) leave out origins already below the line and CRPS uses every origin. The point forecast decides nothing; it is shown for information only, shifted like the median.

### 2026-11-02: Experiment 4 result on the synthetic sets: no variant passes; Berka not scored; nothing adopted
Run `4c77498` on the 20 development personas of each synthetic set (800 origins each), the saved futures read from the `lookahead-runs` worktree and unchanged (40 of 40 hashes). Report: `docs/results/combination_2026-11-02.md` (and `.json`). The futures-store gate passed first: 800 of 800 origins on each synthetic set and 8,000 of 8,000 on Berka equal the experiment 3 records. Origins that get a learned weight, counted from the dates before scoring: (ii) 680 of 800, (iii) 560 of 800 (28 of 40 per persona), on each set.
- **(i) fixed 50/50** fails 7 of the 8 synthetic conditions on both sets (only condition 1 passes): it hurts every archetype where the forecaster beats "balance stays" (mean CRPS $900.89 to $990.21 on the 12-month set, $816.32 to $935.54 on the 24-month set).
- **(ii) one global weight** learned w = 1 at every origin of the 24-month set, so it equals the default there: it passes no harm, the band, CRPS skill, dips caught and Brier, and fails the gain (3) and "the mean CRPS falls" (11), as a no-op must. On the 12-month set it moved 15 percent of origins (mean w 0.97 to 0.99) and fails conditions 2 to 7 and 11.
- **(iii) per-account weights** pass CRPS skill (5) and dips caught (6) on both sets and Brier (7) on the 24-month set, but fail no harm (2), the gain (3: only FREE is net better), the band (4) and the mean CRPS (11) on both: the 30-day error falls a little (-0.9 and -1.8 percent over archetypes) while the absolute lowest-balance bias grows (+34.8 and +19.7 percent).
- **This is what I predicted** on 2026-10-17: the 50/50 fails no harm, the global weight lands near 1 and is close to a no-op, the per-account weights give small CRPS gains in FREE and ITIGHT and then miss the gain condition. Nothing looks too good, so there was no leakage check to make.
- **By the rule nothing is adopted, Berka is not scored for experiment 4, and the forecaster default stays as it is.** Variants counted: 3. Any other combination (scaling the futures, replacing a share, another weighting) would be a new counted variant with its own rule.

### 2026-11-10: Part (b): the possible-subscription recall (56 of 56) is not credible; why link stays pending
The part (b) result (2026-10-23, above) gives rent a precision of 44 percent (11 of 25) and a recall of 43 percent (13 of 30), and possible subscriptions a recall of 100 percent (56 of 56 events over the 40 development personas). **That possible-subscription recall is not credible:** the scorer's "possible" match rule accepts a proposal on any planted subscription of the persona, not on the event's own subscription (`eval/proposals_v2.py` lines 172 to 174 at tag `proposals-v2-final`), so one proposal can count as recovering several events. No decision changes, since possible already fails on precision (56 of 188, 30 percent). A corrected scorer would be a new counted variant; I am not rescoring now.
- **Link: the leakage check of rule (e) was done.** Checked read-only on 2026-10-23: nothing in the link proposer exists only because of the planter; the proposer (806d997) predates the planter (9be3683), and the later refactor (f359a95) did not change its behaviour. Re-checked in the code on 2026-11-10: reads rows posted after the origin: no; reads the answer key: no. **Link stays pending for another reason:** the planted renames all have the same easy shape (same amount and cadence, the old series stops one period before the new one starts, one rename per persona), so 24 of 24 only shows that it works on clean renames.

## Demo and safe-to-spend (written 2026-10-11 on branch `demo`, ported onto `prepublic-prep` on 2026-11-10)

- 2026-11-10: these four entries come from the `demo` branch (tip e8f1073), whose history is separate from main's. I ported the features by hand onto main's stable series ids; the entries are unchanged except where marked. The Time Machine snapshots under `data/demo/timemachine` were regenerated from synthetic data on main's code, so the numbers they hold are not the ones the entries' period had.

### 2026-10-11: Safe-to-spend: method, written before the code
- **What it says.** On the Forecast page and Home: "About $Z/day for everyday spending until <date> keeps you above your $<buffer> buffer, with 80% confidence", labelled an estimate, with the usual everyday spending ($Y/day) beside it. It only calls the forecaster; nothing in `lookahead/forecast/` changes.
- **The window.** From the first forecast day to the day before the next expected income: the first money-in payment of an active income series placed after the first forecast day (planned deposits are not income here). With no such payment inside 30 days, the window is 30 days and the sentence says "for the next 30 days".
- **The futures.** The forecast is built as usual (`build_forecast`, the weekday estimate, as of the forecast date, so rule zero holds as it does for the forecast) with its horizon set to the window's length. A level of Z cents a day is a what-if in the existing scenario engine, so it uses the same random draws as the forecast (common random numbers): the drawn everyday spending is cut by 100 percent over the window (card purchases, cash, transfers out in no series; returns, recurring payments, planned items, fees and big payments that keep coming stay as forecast), and a purchase of Z is added on every day of the window as an entered item (it stays on its day).
- **Safe** means that at least 80 percent of the futures stay at or above the buffer on every day of the window: the scenario's chance of dipping below the buffer is at most 20 percent ("below" is strictly under the buffer, as everywhere in the app).
- **The search.** Spending more lowers every future on every day (same draws), so "safe" can only switch from yes to no as Z rises. If Z = 0 is not safe, there is no safe amount and the page says so plainly: "No safe amount: a dip below your buffer is likely before <date>", where <date> is the first window day by which more than 20 percent of the futures with no everyday spending have dipped (found by bisection on the window's length). Otherwise the upper end starts at $50 a day and doubles until it is not safe (at most $100,000 a day), and bisection on whole cents finds the largest safe Z exactly. The page shows it rounded down to whole dollars, so rounding never moves it into the unsafe side.
- **The usual everyday spending** is the forecast's own everyday-spending estimate (the weekday average), averaged over the window's days.
- **Tests:** a higher buffer never raises the number; already below the buffer gives "no safe amount" with a date; no income expected gives a 30-day window; the search equals the exact answer computed another way (each future's own largest safe level from its zero-spending path, then the level that 80 percent of futures can afford); rule zero (rows after the forecast date change nothing).
- **Honesty check (optional, if time allows), written now:** the 20 development personas of the 12-month set (never held-out ones), the 40 weekly backtest origins. At each origin: the safe amount as of the origin; the actual everyday spending a day over the window (the same stream the forecaster calls everyday spending, read from the rows after the origin, classified with the rows up to the window's end); whether the observed closing balance stayed at or above the buffer on every day of the window. Reported: among origins with a safe amount and actual spending at or below it, the share that stayed above the buffer (it should be about 80 percent or more), and the same for origins that spent more, with n (personas, origins). If the first share is well under 80 percent, I say so; nothing is tuned to it.

### 2026-10-11: Safe-to-spend shown as "plenty of room" above 3 times the usual spending; the local-only rule moves from the config file into code
- **Plenty of room (my request).** When the safe amount is more than 3 times the usual everyday spending (and the usual is above zero), the card does not lead with a big daily figure, which reads as an invitation to spend it. It says "Plenty of room: you could spend well above your usual $Y/day and stay above your $B buffer until <date> (80% confidence)", with the exact figure in smaller text. At 3 times or less, the daily figure leads as before. The threshold is a presentation choice, not measured.
- **Local-only, enforced in code instead of the config file.** Streamlit Community Cloud reads exactly one config file, `.streamlit/config.toml` at the repository root ("you can only have one configuration file", Streamlit's file-organization page), and the options it overrides do not include `server.address`. So `address = "localhost"` in that file would also bind the public demo to localhost, and a demo-only config file is not possible. Decision: the line leaves the config file, and every page checks the address it is served on (in `apply`): outside demo mode, if it is not `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or `::1`, the page shows how to start it locally and stops before reading any data. This is stricter than before (a `--server.address` flag could override the config silently; now it cannot). Locally I start the app with `streamlit run app/Home.py --server.address localhost`, or put `address = "localhost"` under `[server]` in my own `~/.streamlit/config.toml` once. Demo mode (synthetic data only) may be served on any address. This replaces "the server listens on localhost only" in config (2026-09-30, Transactions page).

### 2026-10-11: Demo mode for a public server (Streamlit Community Cloud): design, written before the code
- **Switch.** `LOOKAHEAD_DEMO=1` (an environment variable; on Community Cloud a root-level secret, which Streamlit turns into an environment variable when the server starts). Off by default: nothing changes for my local app.
- **Synthetic only.** The data choice offers "Synthetic demo" only; "My data" is gone. A guard makes it impossible to open anything under `data/private` in demo mode: an audit hook (Python's `sys.addaudithook`) refuses every file open, SQLite connection, directory listing and glob whose path lies in a `data/private` folder. It is installed when the `lookahead` package is imported with the switch on (and again by every page), checks the switch at each event, and cannot be removed for the life of the process. Belt and braces: `private_db_path()` refuses in demo mode, and the Model report does not look for real results.
- **No keys, no .env.** In demo mode `.env` is never read (`load_env` returns nothing without opening the file), the pseudonym key is a fixed made-up demo key (the demo personas' names are synthetic; the key only keeps "Pseudonymize text" working), and the LLM fallback is off: no API key is looked for, and sending is refused in code.
- **No import.** `python -m lookahead.ingest` refuses to run in demo mode; the pages have no upload.
- **Banner.** Every page shows "Synthetic demo data" at the top (in `apply`, which every page calls first). Reworded on 2026-11-18 to "**Demo with made-up data** · every account and transaction here is generated for the demo. Importing bank files and the optional LLM feature are turned off." In demo mode the top bar has no "Data to ..." chip (my own data keeps it; test). In demo mode the app's "today" is the day after the demo data ends (`demo_mode.today`), so no line compares the made-up data with the real date (the Forecast page's "Today is" line, Recurring's "before today" note); my real data keeps the real clock (tests).
- **Time Machine.** The steps of the seven demo personas (the three demo personas and four development personas of the mix: TIGHT-03, ITIGHT-01, FREE-01, SAL-01; never held-out ones) are precomputed with `python -m lookahead.timemachine_snapshots` and committed in `data/demo/timemachine` with each persona's small database (for the what-if), so the free server never builds a year of forecasts. Saved steps hold counts, cents and dates only (no names, keys or account). In demo mode the page reads that folder.
- **Known limits, accepted:** visitors share one demo database, so a confirmation clicked by one visitor is seen by the next until the app restarts; the demo database is built on the first visit (a minute or so).
- **Tests:** in a separate Python process with the switch on: opening, connecting to, listing or globbing anything under `data/private` fails; `.env` is never opened even when one exists; every page runs from a folder with no `.env`, shows the banner, and opens nothing under `data/private` (checked by recording every open).

### 2026-10-11: Safe-to-spend: result of the honesty check (synthetic development personas)
`python -m eval.safe_to_spend_check`, table in `docs/results/safe_to_spend_check.md`. 12-month set, 20 development personas, 40 weekly origins (800).
- **Origins with a safe amount where the actual everyday spending was at or below it: 98 percent stayed at or above the buffer every day of the window (665 of 678, 20 personas).** Above the 80 percent the amount promises. Where spending went above it: 18 percent (7 of 39, 9 personas). With no safe amount: 2 percent (2 of 83, 7 personas). By archetype the first share is 100 percent for DHOME (117 of 117, 3 personas), FREE (40 of 40, 1 persona), IFAM (120 of 120, 3 personas), SAL (120 of 120, 3 personas) and SEM (40 of 40, 1 persona), 97 percent for DRENT (87 of 90, 3 personas), 99 percent for TIGHT (72 of 73, 3 personas) and 88 percent for ITIGHT (69 of 78, 3 personas).
- **Read with care.** This says the amount is not optimistic; it does not say it is calibrated. The median safe amount is 10.6 times the usual everyday spending (n = 717), so nearly every origin spent far less than it and the check rarely looks near the edge, where the 80 percent would be tested. That also supports showing "plenty of room" rather than a big figure. Before this full run I had looked at one persona (v2-TIGHT-01: 30 of 31) while timing the script; nothing was changed after either.

### 2026-10-11: The Forecast page tells the truth about arrivals and unplaced series; "income assumed" only for placed income (my code review, written before the code)
- **Why.** Since keeps-coming arrivals and fixes (a) and (b) became the default, two sentences on the Forecast page are false: big deposits are "never drawn" and big rows are "not repeated in the forecast". Big rows that keep coming at a similar size are drawn as random arrivals in the futures. And the page says "income assumed" when an income series exists, even if (b) chose not to place it (a salary that possibly ended), so the page claims income the forecast does not contain.
- **`has_income`** (`lookahead/forecast/path.py`, the one change in the forecaster, at my request) is computed over the series that are placed: an active, not rejected money-in series that (a) or (b) did not take out. Nothing the futures or the point forecast compute changes; only this flag, which the page, the Time Machine's saved steps and the backtest records read. The committed Time Machine steps keep the old flag; the Time Machine page does not show it, so I do not remake them now.
- **The Forecast carries what was not placed**: a new field with each unplaced series' key and the reason, "weekly card purchases" for (a) (their rows are everyday spending again) or "possibly ended" for (b) (last payment more than 1.5 periods before the data's last day). Read from what `build_forecast` already computes; nothing new is decided.
- **The page.** In the assumptions: how many recurring-size big deposits (and payments) of the last 365 days are drawn as random arrivals in the simulated futures, at their observed rate and sizes (so the range, the middle future and the chances include them); how many recurring-size payments stay set aside because there are fewer than 6 a year. A line lists the recurring series not placed and why. The two false sentences are replaced: the rows listed as set aside are not drawn, and big rows that keep coming are not listed there because they are drawn as arrivals.
- **Tests, failing first:** a possibly ended income series gives `has_income` False under (b) and True with the fix off, and the Forecast lists it as not placed; the page states the arrivals count and that they are drawn, lists a weekly card series as not placed, and no longer contains "never drawn" or "not repeated in the forecast".

### 2026-11-17: Safe-to-spend near the edge: the reading rule, written before the run
- **Why.** The honesty check above (98 percent, 665 of 678 origins, 20 personas) rarely tests the amount where it matters: the median safe amount is 10.6 times the usual everyday spending (n = 717), so nearly every origin spent far below it. A reviewer flagged the 98 percent as too good to read on its own. I chose to add a row near the edge.
- **The run.** `python -m eval.safe_to_spend_check`, unchanged in method: the 20 synthetic development personas of the 12-month set (never held-out ones), the 40 recorded weekly origins (800), on the code of this branch. The forecaster has changed since 2026-10-11, so the whole table is run again; it goes to a new dated file, `docs/results/safe_to_spend_check_2026-11-17.md`, and the 2026-10-11 file is not changed.
- **Near the edge.** An origin with a safe amount where the safe amount is at most 2 times the usual everyday spending (the usual above zero). Fixed now, not chosen after seeing the result.
- **The row.** Near-the-edge origins whose actual everyday spending was at or below the safe amount: the share that stayed at or above the buffer every day of the window, with the number of origins and of personas. Also the near-the-edge origins that spent above it, with their n.
- **Reading rule.** Fewer than 30 near-the-edge origins at or below the amount, or fewer than 5 personas among them: "too few origins near the edge to say", with the n. Otherwise: 80 percent or more stayed above, "the 80 percent holds near the edge"; from 70 to under 80 percent, "slightly optimistic near the edge"; under 70 percent, "optimistic near the edge: the 80 percent does not hold there".
- **After the run.** The row, its n and its reading go into the README as they are. Nothing is tuned afterwards: the safe-to-spend method, the 2-times line, the 80 percent target and the "plenty of room" threshold stay as they are. A different whole-table result from 2026-10-11 is reported as it is.

### 2026-11-17: Safe-to-spend near the edge: the result, read by the rule above
`python -m eval.safe_to_spend_check`, 20 synthetic development personas of the 12-month set, 800 origins; table in `docs/results/safe_to_spend_check_2026-11-17.md`.
- **Near the edge (a safe amount at most 2 times the usual), spending at or below it: 89 percent stayed at or above the buffer (31 of 35 origins, 10 personas).** By the rule written before the run (at least 30 origins and 5 personas, then 80 percent or more): "the 80 percent holds near the edge". Near the edge, spending above the amount: 16 percent (6 of 38, 9 personas).
- **Read with care.** 35 origins is only just above the 30 the rule asks for, and they overlap within 10 personas; with that n the share could plausibly be anywhere from the mid-70s to the mid-90s percent. So this says the amount is not clearly optimistic where it is tight, not that it is calibrated.
- **The rest of the table is unchanged from 2026-10-11** on this branch's code: 98 percent (665 of 678, 20 personas) at or below the amount, 18 percent (7 of 39, 9 personas) above it, 2 percent (2 of 83, 7 personas) with no safe amount. Nothing was tuned after the run.

- **The merge.** Main (803a7e1: scenario v2 and the navigation rework) merged into `prepublic-prep` with a merge commit, not a rebase, so the pushed branch keeps its history. The public demo shares one database among its visitors, so a balance one visitor types (possibly their own) would be shown to the next. Chosen: in demo mode the balance is kept in the visitor's own session only and never written. Lost: listing it as one more known limit beside shared confirmations, because a confirmation is a click on made-up data, while a typed balance can be a visitor's own number. Outside demo mode nothing changes.

### 2026-11-17: The 2026-10-11 Forecast page entry ported onto `prepublic-prep` (from `demo`, e8f1073)
- **Ported as written:** `has_income` over placed series only, the new `not_placed` field, and the Forecast page lines (arrivals drawn, payments set aside, series not placed, the two false sentences replaced). The series key is still what (a) and (b) use, so nothing needed changing for the stable series ids. Tests first, as in the original: both failed before the code and pass after.
